Finally, we note that because the zeroth-order flow was
restricted to be field parallel, U = Uˆb, any fluctuation- or
rotation-induced perpendicular flow must appear explic-
itly as the second-order mean U⊥2 ≡⟨U (2)
⊥⟩. All fluxes
will therefore be evaluated including to this fluctuation-
induced flow.
Its value is not obtained from a simple
evolution law but from the implicit solvability condition
(A102), which expresses the coupling of U⊥2 to the global
balance of stress divergences, rotation, and perpendicu-
lar pressure forces. We will assume that the sources Sρ,
SρU, and Sth have no transport-order part, though this
could be straightforwardly included if desired.
b.
Overview of the procedure
We wish to obtain turbulent-driven fluxes of the phys-
ically interesting quantities for solar-coronal modeling —
density, heat, momentum, and magnetic field — as well
as understanding how free energy moves between fluctu-
ations (e.g., Alfv´en waves) and the background equilib-
rium. The first of these goals is partially fulfilled via the
third-order expansion and average of Eqs. (A59)–(A62),
yielding, for instance, the density flux from Eq. (A59), or
the momentum flux and fluctuation-induced forces from
Eq. (A60).
However, for the heating and heat flux — arguably
the most physically important quantity in the theory —
this straightforward average yields transport equations
that still involve the undetermined dissipation rate Dmech
and pressure-work term ⟨p∇· u⟩. To make progress, we
compute the evolution of the mean energies in two ways:
first, by forming the transport equation for Emech and
Eth directly from the mean-conserved variables (ρU, B,
ρ, and σ), thereby eliminating contributions from the
fluctuations; second, by directly averaging ∂Emech/∂t
(Eq. (A65)) and ∂Eth/∂t (Eq. (A4)), which are mean
accumulative and thus retain the fluctuations’ contribu-
tions. The comparison of these calculations then yields
a number of useful results: (i) it allows one to solve for
Dmech and ⟨p∇· u⟩thereby closing the transport sys-
tem; (ii) it proves that the dissipation of fluctuations
is perpendicularly local, viz., there is no perpendicular
transport of fluctuation energy at this order; and (iii) it
provides a stringent algebraic consistency check by recov-
ering the second-order fluctuating energy conservation
law (A54).
Along the way, the calculation reveals the
physical origin of each of the different fluctuating energy
source terms in Eq. (A56). Total energy conservation is
assured from the outset by Eq. (A63).
With this structure in mind, we organize the calcu-
lation by computing the ∂ρ/∂τ, ∂B/∂τ, and ∂(ρU)/∂τ
evolution in §A 5 c, §A 5 d, and §A 5 e, respectively. In
§A 5 g, their energies are combined to form the mechan-
ical energy evolution, then compared to the average of
Eq. (A65) to solve for Dmech.
§A 5 g then derives the
mean thermal energy transport from ∂σ/∂τ and ∂ρ/∂τ,
and, by direct analogy with Emech, compares this with
the average of Eq. (A4) to solve for ⟨p∇· u⟩. Combining
all of these results yields the local energy conservation
law and the total heating rate and heat flux. We sum-
marize and provide a detailed physical interpretation of
the various effects in §A 5 j.
c.
Density transport
Expanding the continuity law to third order and aver-
aging gives the stationary-frame density flux:
∂ρ
∂τ = −∇· (⟨δρ δu⊥⟩+ ρ U⊥2) = −∇· (ρ eVρ),
(A70)
where we define the density’s turbulent advection veloc-
ity, eVρ = Vρ + U⊥2 with ρVρ = ⟨δρ δu⊥⟩, for use be-
low. The flux is purely perpendicular because parallel
momentum density — not u∥itself — is treated as mean-
conserved, enforcing ⟨u(2)
∥⟩= −

δu∥δρ

/ρ such that the
turbulent parallel transport vanishes.
d.
Magnetic-field transport
The induction equation averaged to transport order
reads
∂B
∂τ = ∇× ⟨δu × δB⟩+ ∇× (U⊥2 × B),
(A71)
where we have dropped the average of the resistive dissi-
pation term, by assuming it acts only on the small scales.
The first term here involves the turbulent electromotive
force E ≡⟨δu × δB⟩commonly discussed in mean-field
dynamo theory [168]. Because the perpendicular part of
E can be written as E⊥= V × B for some V , its effect
can be understood like an effective velocity and therefore


## Page 54


54
conserves flux by Alfv´en’s theorem. In contrast, the par-
allel part E∥= ˆb · E is not of this form, and can act as
an anomalous resistivity that enhances the diffusion of
the large-scale field (the so-called “β effect” in dynamo
theory). We prove below that E∥vanishes, a nontrivial
consequence of the RMHD ordering and incompressibil-
ity.
Decomposing δu = δu⊥+ˆb δu∥and δB = δB⊥+ˆb δB∥
gives three types of cross terms, two of which vanish.
a.
Parallel-parallel products
First, δu∥ˆb × δB∥ˆb = 0
trivially, so there are no contributions from products of
parallel fluctuations.
b.
Perpendicular-perpendicular products
The term
δu⊥× δB⊥is more subtle.
At this order both fields
are perpendicular and divergence-free, so we write them
in terms of the scalar potentials Φ, Ψ (Eq. (A37)). Then
δu⊥× δB⊥= ˆb√ρ {Φ, Ψ}
(A72)
where {Φ, Ψ} ≡ˆb · (∇Φ × ∇Ψ), viz., the contribution
is parallel to ˆb with its magnitude given by the Pois-
son bracket of the two scalar potentials. The turbulence
average of the Poisson bracket is itself a perpendicular
boundary term (the perpendicular integral of the Pois-
son bracket vanishes in a homogeneous domain) imply-
ing that ⟨δu⊥× δB⊥⟩= ˆb√4πρ ⟨{Φ, Ψ}⟩= O(ϵ3). Tak-
ing the curl adds another order in ϵ (ˆb√4πρ ⟨{Φ, Ψ}⟩is
a large-scale quantity), so the contribution to (A71) is
O(ϵ4) and can be dropped at transport order.
Physically, since an E∥is needed to break field lines,
this is the RMHD version of the statement from Abel
et al. [43] that there that is no mean-field anomalous re-
sistivity driven by fluctuations in gyrokinetics. In that
theory, this physics is contained within the evolution of
the safety factor q (their equation (143)), which cannot be
enhanced by the turbulent fluctuations. Here, it appears
in a more general form without restricting the geometry
to closed flux surfaces. In particular, it seems that the
perpendicular incompressibility of RMHD, together with
the enforced scale separation, forbids the Richardson-
type explosive separation of neighbouring field lines. This
appears to break a key assumption behind the stochastic-
flux-freezing proofs of Eyink et al. [169] and therefore
precludes the enhanced “reconnection-diffusion” resistiv-
ity posited in Lazarian et al. [170].

should not drive significant dissipation of the large-scale
momentum.

flux ρU(VU + eVρ). We also see some familiar terms from
past solar-wind literature, particularly the role of gra-
dients in the Alfv´en-wave pressure from W ⊥

residual energy term (which can be written z+·z−∇· ˆb).
These effects are generalized by Eq. (A77) to also capture
forces and parallel transport from compressive fluctua-
tions, which are likely unimportant compared to those of
Alfv´enic fluctuations in the solar-wind context.

i.
Perpendicular flow
Throughout our derivation we have kept the second-
order fluctuation-induced perpendicular flow U⊥2 gen-
eral. Its special status arises from our original assump-
tion on the lowest-order flow as being parallel U = Uˆb,
meaning that ⟨u⊥⟩is not a dynamically evolving field,
but rather evolves as a constraint for the application of
multiple-time-scale analysis, viz., the system must self-
generate U⊥2 to yield a system that is quasi-stationary
on the fast timescale.
We can understand this constraint by starting with the
perpendicular momentum equation (A2), which at lowest

Practically, this suggests a natural strategy: evolve the
mean transport system (for ρ, ρU, Eth, and B) with
U⊥2 chosen to ensure that the perpendicular equilib-
rium (A99) remains satisfied on the updated profiles (this
likely requires an iterative solver of some sort).
This
is closely analogous to the fusion practice of working
in flux coordinates: one solves for an equilibrium mag-
netic geometry (e.g. via Grad–Shafranov in axisymme-
try), evolves transport on those surfaces, and only then
(if needed) reconstructs the implied motion of the flux
surfaces; in our notation, that “surface motion” is eVψ.
The transport equations are recast in this form below
(see Eq. (A103)).

The terms involving ˆb, κ, ∇· ˆb, ∇ˆb, and S arise from
commuting the projector through the divergence, a sim-
ilar origin to the F ⊥
RM parallel Maxwell/Reynolds stress
introduced for Eq. (A77).
It is important to emphasize the interpretation of
(A102), which does not remove the need to enforce the
perpendicular force-balance constraint to compute U⊥2:
it is simply a convenient along-ˆb form of the same con-
dition.
Consequently, any U⊥2 that maintains R⊥=
0 along the entire field line will automatically satisfy
(A102), because (A102) was explictly derived by rewrit-
ing that constraint. What (A102) does provide is a po-
tentially useful shortcut for solution: one may determine
U⊥2 at a single ℓthen integrate (A102) to reconstruct its
parallel structure along the field line, rather than solving
for U⊥2 from R⊥(ℓ) at each ℓ. It also helps illustrate var-
ious physical effects, for instance how the Coriolis force
(the final term 2ρΩ×U) will drive the nascent beginnings
of the Parker spiral in U⊥2, as described by Weber and
Davis [5]; that said, once this grows to larger angles in
super-Alfv´enic regions the ordering U⊥2 ∼ϵ2 will break
down and the theory ceases to apply.
Finally, note the special behaviour as U →0: in that
limit the explicit U⊥2 terms on the left-hand side van-
ish, reflecting the fact that the perpendicular momentum
constraint reduces to a balance between turbulent quan-
tities (perpendicular stresses and pressure forces), while
U⊥2 becomes underdetermined by (A102) alone. This is
not a contradiction: it is the statement that when the
mean parallel advection is absent, the constraint fixes
an instantaneous force balance but does not uniquely
determine the “label velocity” of the large-scale geom-
etry. Closely related degeneracies (and their resolution
only at higher order, or via global/ambipolarity con-
straints) are familiar in low-flow gyrokinetic transport
theory [200, 201].
j.
Summary of the third-order system
The averaged third-order system has yielded a set of
evolution equations for the slow-timescale evolution of
density (A70), magnetic field (A74), momentum (A77),
and thermal energy (A90). Each of these are split into a
turbulence-driven flux — the part inside a total deriva-
tive – and, for the momentum and heat, additional
forces/sources. With a closure or simulations of how the
fluctuations depend on the large-scale gradients, as cap-
tured by the second-order system, these equations could
be evolved in time to yield the slow evolution of the
mean background due to injected waves or self-generated
instabilities. Subject to a few caveats discussed below
in App. B they yield equations for a Parker-like wind
with additional effects from wave pressure, wave heat-
ing, and cross-field turbulent transport of particles, field,
momentum and heat. Total energy conservation on the
transport time scale is ensured from the outset by our
method; the mechanical and thermal parts sum to yield


## Page 59


59
the third-order expansion of the total energy Eq. (A63),
which — as a total divergence — has no sources other
than the external ones. Thus, although the various terms
involving compressive products that appear are likely to
be very small and physically unimportant in most situa-
tions (e.g., 2W ∥
r ˆb·∇U), perhaps warranting their neglect
in any practical implementation, their inclusion is crucial
for providing overall consistency to the theory.
The magnetic-field transport equation (A74) revealed
that B is transported perpendicularly in the plasma
frame at net velocity Vψ, implying magnetic surfaces
move at speed eVψ ≡Vψ+U⊥2 in the zeroth-order station-
ary frame. In keeping with the convention of gyrokinetic
theories [41–43] it is natural to choose to measure fluxes
with respect to eVψ.
Physically, this is transparent —
fluxes of density, heat, and momentum are “cross-field”
fluxes — it also has the advantage of removing the ex-
plicit appearance of U⊥2 in perpendicular fluxes, which
may be difficult to determine in some geometries. In Abel
et al. [43], where the magnetic field is used to define the
radial coordinate, this choice appears in the commutation
of the time derivative through the flux-surface average:
this corresponds to either the Vψ term or the ∂ψ/∂t con-
tribution in their equations (42) or (45).
With this choice, we write each of the transport equa-
tions in the form

In deriving Eq. (A107) we have inserted the fluctua-

thermal-driving terms, then rearranged terms between

This form, which has explicitly removed the heating from
fluctuations in favor of the conservation law (A97) that
arises from the assumed quasi-stationarity, captures ex-
plicitly the fluctuation-mediated transfer of kinetic, po-

of Sth). On the other hand, it hides the relevance of ther-

th (A91)
— any energy deposited into fluctuations is immediately
(on the transport timescale) dissipated back into heat,
thereby driving transport but not heating.
The fluctuation-induced forces (SρU) and heating (Sth)
provide a complementary physical picture to the discus-
sion of the factors influencing the local heating rate below
Eq. (A97). In the force SρU we have (in order of appear-
ance in (A106)):
(i) The force on the mean flow from Reynolds and
Maxwell stresses of Alfv´enic fluctuations, which
arises from the expanding (∇·ˆb) or shearing (S) ge-
ometry;
(ii) The “redirection” of the cross-field momentum
transport along curved field lines (likely unimpor-
tant);
(iii) The parallel force from gradients of the perturbed
total thermal and magnetic pressure;
(iv) The Reynolds and Maxwell stresses of parallel fluc-
tuations; and


## Page 60

(v) The centrifugal force.
In the fluctuation-induced heating Sth we have (in order
of appearance in (A107)):
(i) Heating from the compression of the plasma in the
magnetic-field frame (note that there is some flex-
ibility in this term, which can be changed in form
by changing Γth; we follow Abel et al. [43] equation
(194));
(ii) Heating, enabled by exchange with the fluctuations,
from the relaxation of perpendicular gradients of the
mean flow;
(iii) Heating, enabled by the fluctuations, from the free
energy lost/released when mass is moved across the
effective gravitational potential;
(iv) The dissipation (or growth) of fluctuation free en-
ergy as it propagates through the domain in the
parallel direction, mediated by an adiabatic ex-
change of thermal fluctuations with the background

(vii) Corrections to the standard thermal heat flux due

(viii) The fluctuation’s contribution to the work done by

As described in the main text, the source of the fluc-
tuations that yield non-zero Vψ, VU, Vρ, Alfv´enic ener-
gies, or parallel correlations can be either from exter-
nally driven waves propagating through the domain, or
from self-generated instabilities. The latter, a subset of
which are explored below in App. D, has been the focus
of fusion-related studies, while the former seems likely to
be more important in understanding coronal heating.
Appendix B: Solving multiscale RMHD as a 3-D
heliospheric model
Here we provide further commentary on the mathe-
matics and interpretation of multiscale RMHD, towards
the purpose of explaining how a global model of the he-
liosphere could be constructed and evolved in time as
described in § II E.
a.
How a Parker-like wind appears in the multiscale system.
The multiscale RMHD expansion contains a standard
field-aligned “Parker wind” already in the equilibrium
at O(ϵ). In particular, the parallel momentum balance
(13), together with mass (and flux) conservation along
the field, ˆb ·∇(ρU/B) = 0 (K∥
ρ + K∥
U −K∥
B = 0), form
a closed 1D system for (ρ, U, p) along a given field line.
With the standard transport-time ordering, these rela-
tions are best viewed as instantaneous field-aligned con-
straints that reconstruct (ρ, U, p) along each tube using
ordinary differential equations from lower boundary con-
ditions.
More concretely, following Parker [7], we can assume
isothermality with c2
s = p/ρ = const., γ = 1, and Sρ = 0,
meaning K∥
p = K∥
ρ = K∥
B −K∥
U. The parallel projection
of the O(ϵ) momentum equation (13) then becomes
(U 2 −c2

(B1)
which, if the field is locally radial (ˆb·∇= ∂/∂ℓ= ∂/∂R),
is the standard Parker equation generalized to an arbi-

−K∥
B (note that ∇· ˆb = 2/R for a purely radial field).
This illustrates how the general field-line geometry does
not change the basic wind physics.
a.
Interpretation of the transport system.
As pre-
sented in App. A, the parallel and perpendicular dynam-
ics in the transport system must be treated in funda-
mentally different ways. On the slow time τ, the equa-
tions evolve a field-line-labeled perpendicular structure
in 2D by updating flux-tube-integrated budgets (mass,
momentum, and thermal energy). In contrast, the field-
aligned profiles along each flux tube are slaved con-
straints: at each transport step they are reconstructed
instantaneously from the parallel equilibrium and bound-
ary conditions, rather than being advanced as local-
in-ℓinitial-value equations.
This is the same organiz-
ing principle used in multiscale gyrokinetics [42, 43],
where the procedure would be to compute perpendicu-
lar mass/momentum/heat fluxes, evolve the flux-surface-
averaged fields on the transport time, then reconstruct
the 2D equilibrium via a Grad–Shafranov solve.
In
our open-field setting, the reconstruction would also in-
volve the 1D Parker-like boundary-value problem along ˆb
(Eq. (B1)), and the underlying scale-separation assump-
tion is that information can propagate along the tube (at
speeds ∼U plus the relevant wave speeds) rapidly com-
pared to the time over which perpendicular transport
significantly modifies the field-line-labeled state.
Within this viewpoint, the second-order mean perpen-
dicular flow U⊥2 plays a central role in explaining the
different roles of the parallel and perpendicular equilib-
ria at O(ϵ): U⊥2 is not prescribed a priori, but is instead
determined by the requirement that the averaged sys-
tem remain slowly evolving, which requires it satisfy the
perpendicular momentum balance at each transport step
(see § A 5 i for further discussion). A straightforward ap-
proach to achieve this, taken by fusion transport solvers,
is to frame the transport system in terms of cross-field,
as opposed to stationary-frame, fluxes (41), then use the
equilibrium to infer U⊥2 if it is needed directly.


## Page 61


61
b.
A difficulty: the parallel equilibrium constraints
An attentive reader will nevertheless have noticed a
potentially serious issue: in the strict multiscale order-
ing (A10) the leading-order mean solution is adiabatic
(K∥
s = 0) unless external sources are imposed, with
the wave/turbulent dissipation that is crucial for driving
faster wind entering the thermal-energy evolution only
on the transport time (schematically ∂Eth/∂τ in (39)).
Likewise, wave-pressure forces are absent in the momen-
tum equation. A strict interpretation of the multiscale
system would spread any heat/momentum added across
the full length of the tube in question, disallowing local-
ized forces or heating effects — the corresponding solu-
tions will disagree strongly with observations.
This mismatch is related to both the 2+1D interpreta-
tion discussed above and the fluctuation ordering: stan-
dard RMHD assumes z+/cs ≪1, whereas a wave-driven
wind generally involves a z+ whose energy is comparable
to the enthalpy change required to accelerate the wind
over an expansion length (W +
⊥∼Eth, or z+ ∼cs). In-
deed, in the solar corona and wind, it is often the case
that z+ ∼cs while still maintaining δρ/ρ ≪1 (i.e. large
transverse motions without strong compressibility). In
this regime, it is not realistic to treat wave heating as a
small correction to an otherwise adiabatic equilibrium.
a.
Robustness of the turbulent dynamics.
Impor-
tantly, this mismatch is primarily a statement about the
mean ordering — i.e., about the cumulative heating and
acceleration produced by dissipation over large distances
— rather than about the utility of the fluctuation equa-
tions and the local phenomenologies developed above.
This can be understood more formally by considering
an alternative low-β, imbalanced ordering, as developed
in App. C. There, we consider a transonic outward wave
with z+ ∼cs ≪vA, while still requiring δρ/ρ ≪1,
and show that the resulting fluctuation equations are
precisely the appropriate low-β subsidiary limits of the
system derived from the standard ordering (9). In this
sense, the asymptotic procedures commute at the level of
the fluctuation equations: starting from full MHD with
transonic, low-β, highly imbalanced fluctuations gives the
same reduced fluctuation dynamics as taking the low-β
limit of the present RMHD system.
This logic is, moreover, closely aligned with standard
practice in the solar-wind literature. In many applica-
tions, RMHD-like or incompressible Alfv´enic dynamics
are coupled directly to Parker-like backgrounds modified
by wave pressure and dissipation, without insisting on the
formal condition z+ ≪cs [e.g., 1, 26, 72, 117, 202, 203].
The practical requirement is instead that the fluctuations
remain predominantly transverse and only weakly com-
pressive, so that they do not produce shocks or large
density variations. From this perspective, App. C sim-
ply provides a controlled asymptotic justification for a
modeling logic that is already widely used.
However, what does change in the imbalanced low-β
ordering is the partitioning of terms in the mean equa-
tions.
In particular, wave-pressure, thermal-pressure,
and transport/heating terms move between equilibrium
and transport orders, so a strict transport derivation no
longer has the same clean separation as in the standard
multiscale system (see App. C 2).
This repartitioning
suggests that the strict ordering of terms between the
parallel equilibrium (at O(ϵ)) and transport (at O(ϵ3))
is not robust, and might be — with care — profitably
reorganized.
c.
Resummed equilibrium
We now exploit this separation — robust small-scale
dynamics with a cumulatively important mean feedback
— to propose a resummed equilibrium method that al-
lows wave-driven Parker-like solutions within the stan-
dard multiscale RMHD ordering (A10): rather than tak-
ing the adiabatic equilibrium (10) as the lowest-order
background, we re-define the leading-order mean state
to be a 1D, field-aligned wind that already includes a
selection of heating terms and wave-pressure forces. Op-
erationally, this is achieved by retaining the mean system,
Eqs. (A14), (A19) and (A22), but with chosen terms from
the transport equations repartitioned from O(ϵ3) into the
arbitrary sources Sρ, Sth, and SρU at O(ϵ) (similar to
how the low-β ordering of App. C moves terms between
equilibrium and transport orders). This thus “resums”
the parallel equilibrium. The idea is justified by the fact
that the fluctuation equations, and therefore the turbu-
lent dynamics, do not depend explicitly on Sρ, Sth, or SU
themselves; their influence therein comes only through
their effect on changing the background parallel gradi-
ents (K∥
s, K∥
U etc.).
Equivalently, one may regard the
repartitioning as a partial resummation of the perturba-
tion series for the mean profiles, in which terms that are
formally higher order but accumulate coherently along
ˆb, are absorbed into the O(ϵ) sources to define an im-
proved leading-order equilibrium. From the perspective
of conservation laws, this repartitioning is essentially ir-
relevant: parallel variation in Eth or ρU that would have
been fixed by the lowest-order equations is now not, but
the total integrated exchange between background and
fluctuations, or via parallel fluxes in the case of waves,
remains the same.
a.
Practical solution strategies.
In practice, the re-
summed framework can be used in (at least) two closely
related ways.
In both cases, one specifies an (initial)
large-scale geometry of B and other mean quantities,
boundary conditions for the injected outward-wave am-
plitude at the coronal base, and a form for the quadratic
correlators/fluxes as functionals of the local background
gradients and wave energies. The latter could be a phe-
nomenological closure such as that outlined in §III A, or,
more accurately, simulations of the second-order RMHD
equations along flux-tubes.
(i) Fixed-point (steady) resummation.
One seeks a
steady, field-aligned background in which the mean pro-


## Page 62


62
files and the wave dissipation computed from the closure
are mutually consistent.
Operationally, this amounts
to iterating between solving the 1D mean equations
along ˆb for (ρ, U, p) together with the outward-wave
evolution, and updating the resummed sources (Sρ,
SρU = ρSU + USρ, and/or Sth) from the closure un-
til convergence.
Conceptually, this is simply the fa-
miliar flux-tube problem of a wind driven by wave
pressure and wave heating (now augmented by addi-
tional curvature/stratification/transport-mediated chan-
nels), as in standard wave-driven wind models and codes
[e.g., 20, 202].
In this form, the method is most nat-
urally applied when the perpendicular structure is pre-
scribed (or varies only parametrically), so that each field
line may be treated as an approximately independent 1D
problem.
(ii) Running resummation (time-dependent). A partic-
ularly simple alternative is to evolve the slow-time system
directly, continuously evaluating the fluctuation/closure
terms using the current background gradients, rather
than enforcing the formally lowest-order adiabatic paral-
lel equilibrium at each step. In mathematical terms, the
equations solved are simply the sum of the O(ϵ) parallel-
equilibrium constraints and the O(ϵ3) transport correc-
tions (which involves the slow time derivatives), thereby
yielding a single resummed parallel structure [204] Equiv-
alently, one simply considers the mass, parallel momen-
tum and heat sources (Sρ, SρU, and Sth, respectively) to
be the negative of the relevant O(ϵ3) transport equation,
safe with the knowledge that the fluctuation equations
remain valid for arbitrary sources.
The resulting system is then

where in the momentum equation (B4), we have summed
the parallel equilibrium (13) and USρ = −U∇· (ˆbρU)
to obtain an equation for the momentum (as opposed
to the velocity). We have set Sρ = SU = 0, given the
lack of external forces and mass sources in the coronal
context, but the heat source Sth is included due to its
physical importance for capturing radiative cooling. The
system (B2)–(B5) additionally requires the perpendicular
equilibrium (12), which determines U⊥2 as a constraint
(see discussion in § A 5 i).
This “running” procedure, which thus simply amounts
to combining all parallel-structure-related mean equa-
tions before their solution, is attractive because it avoids
any special bookkeeping: the departure of the parallel
mean state from the adiabatic background is produced
self-consistently by the same wave-pressure and heating
terms that appear in the transport equations, and the
steady solution (if one exists) will emerge as the fixed
point of the evolution. Indeed, we can see Eq. (B4) as a
Parker-wind-like equation (similar to (B1)) that includes
the wave pressure [72]. In addition, the system contains
all of the new effects discussed in §III, including from per-
pendicular transport of mass, momentum, and heat, and
heating via the relaxation of perpendicular gradients (the
Y⊥terms). The system therefore provides a direct route
to obtaining realistic wave-driven profiles within the mul-
tiscale framework for a true 3-D heliospheric model. Fur-
ther discussion of related subtleties, particularly how the
ideas connect with explicitly transsonic orderings, is pro-
vided in App. C 2.
b.
Limitations.
We emphasize that the strategy de-
scribed above is best viewed as a practical modeling de-
vice rather than a controlled procedure: it amounts to
a partial resummation in which formally higher-order
terms that accumulate coherently in the parallel struc-
ture are promoted to define an improved leading-order
wind.
Making this logic fully systematic would re-
quire a more formal treatment of this reorganization;
e.g., a modified ordering, as considered in App. C, or
renormalization-group methods for differential equations
(see [205, 206] and references therein). The resulting re-
summed wind need not be unique and may exhibit mul-
tiple branches or instabilities (e.g., multiple transonic so-
lutions or unstable equilibria), so numerical strategies
should be designed to diagnose non-uniqueness and se-
lect the physically relevant branch. Moreover, given that
the method does not preserve the strict ordering, care is
required to ensure the intended scale separation is pre-
served: any terms promoted into the parallel “equilib-
rium” via Sth, SρU, and/or Sρ must be slowly varying in
time and space — there exists the risk of contaminating
the reduced equilibrium problem with fast-time variabil-
ity that lies outside the ordering assumptions. A final
related danger, which is shared by the standard order-
ing of App. A (i.e., it is not related to the resummation
procedure), is that the ordering formally assumes that
the profile is communicated along the full length of a
flux tube on timescales shorter than the transport time;
depending on the heliospheric altitude to which the the
system is solved, this may not be well satisfied.


## Page 63


63
Appendix C: A subsidiary low-β ordering for
transonic, highly imbalanced fluctuations
The ordering of the fluctuations used in the main text
and App. A assumes δz+/cs ≪1.
While this could
be well satisfied in closed and structured boundary re-
gions — where β ∼1 due to the weak field meaning
that Alfv´enic fluctuations are subsonic if |δB⊥|/B < 1
(see Fig. 5; [145]) — in many regions of the corona the
dominant outwards propagating waves are expected to
be transonic (z+ ∼cs). In the main text, we ignored this
issue by arguing that the Alfv´enic fluctuation dynamics
should not depend sensitively on cs itself: these fluctu-
ations are nearly incompressible, suggesting that the re-
duced equations should remain useful even outside their
formal regime of validity, particularly in near-Sun low-β
coronal-hole-like regions where |δB⊥|/B remains small.
Similar assumptions have been regularly made implicitly
in past works that use incompressible or reduced MHD
to model the solar wind [50, 86, 132].
The purpose of this appendix is to move beyond these
qualitative expectations and prove that this is indeed the
case for one choice of ordering for which z+ ∼cs. More
precisely, we show that starting from the full compress-
ible MHD equations with a transonic, highly imbalanced
outward wave that satisfies z+ ∼cs and cs/vA ≪1 leads,
at lowest order, to the same fluctuation system obtained
by taking the low-β limit of the equations derived in §A.
In this sense, the two asymptotic procedures commute:
no new leading-order fluctuation terms appear in a for-
mally valid expansion that are not contained in the stan-
dard RMHD equations and relevant in our slaved phe-
nomenology. This result provides a formal justification
for applying the fluctuation equations from the main text
across both structured subsonic regions and low-β tran-
sonic regions of the corona and solar wind.
Our focus here will be on the fluctuation equations,
relegating a brief discussion of equilibrium and transport
to §C 2 because the mixed ordering yields a system that
does not cleanly separate into different orders (although
the terms remain very similar to those in the main text).
This is sufficient for the practical purpose of the main
text, namely to justify the use of a single fluctuation
model across regions with rather different values of β
and δz+/cs (see § II E and App. B). The ordering pre-
sented below is not unique; it is simply a compact proof
of principle, and further work along these lines would
prove valuable.
1.

l.
Interpretation and limitations
The five fluctu-
ation
equations
Eqs.
(C21),
(C22),
(C25),
(C27)
and (C29), together with the Z(0) equation (C11), are
precisely the relevant β ∼ς2 ≪1, MA ∼ς ≪1, large-
z+ subsidiary limits of the standard fluctuation system
derived in § A; they thus recover the dominant terms in
our slaved phenomenology for imbalanced transonic tur-
bulence. In particular, the passive advection of δB∥/B
is not a new assumption but simply the low-β limit of
the RMHD δB∥equation (A43): in the present order-
ing, its source terms are suppressed by β ∼ς2 and so
drop out at lowest order. By contrast, the δu∥and δρ/ρ
equations remain nontrivial, retaining the couplings to
the Alfv´enic fluctuations that govern density transport,
parallel flows, and low-β, low-MA compressive dynamics.
Thus, although slow-wave-like propagation is demoted in
this limit (they were likewise dropped in our slaved phe-
nomenology), the rest of the compressive subsystem has
survived unchanged.
The key formal result is therefore that the asymptotic
procedures commute at the order considered here: start-
ing from the full compressible MHD equations and ex-
panding for a low-β, low-MA, highly imbalanced, tran-
sonic outward wave yields the same fluctuation equa-
tions as taking the appropriate limit of the RMHD sys-
tem derived above.
This is not obvious a priori.
It
could have failed, for example, if the transonic ordering
had destroyed the perpendicular incompressibility of the
Alfv´enic corrections δz±
⊥, or if additional feedback terms
appeared in the compressive equations from the parallel
(spherically polarized) part of Z(0).
Let us also comment on the interpretation of the
stronger pressure ordering (C17), which does not mean
that magnetic-pressure fluctuations or pressure pertur-
bations must in practice be as large as δB∥/B ∼ϵ and
δp/p ∼ϵ/ς2.
Rather, it shows that if fluctuations of
this size are retained, then they satisfy the passive equa-
tion (C25) in this subsidiary ordering. If instead δp/p is
smaller, then δp and δB∥simply become the O(ςϵ) in-
compressible pressure required to maintain ∇·δu⊥= 0,
while the remaining fluctuation equations still follow so
long as the weaker condition ϵ ≪ς is satisfied so that
δp/p ≪1. Given δu∥and δρ are each driven to their
assumed order by the background gradients, while δB∥
is not, the most natural situation is indeed for δB∥to be
O(ςϵ) and therefore not passively advected.
Finally, the present subsidiary expansion is intended
only as a simple proof of principle, not as the most
general possible ordering. Other consistent choices can
retain additional terms.
For example, taking K⊥
U ∼
ϵ/ς brings the velocity-shear couplings into Eqs. (C27)
and (C29) while remaining fully self-consistent; equiva-
lently, such terms also appear without assuming MA ∼ς.
This argues that the velocity-shear-based ACR heat-
ing discussed in § IV D should be robust, at least
for sub-Alfv´enic fluctuations.
By contrast, taking K⊥
ρ
parametrically larger tends to mix perpendicular-to-
parallel coupling terms, destroying the ordering of δρ/ρ
in the present scheme (this can be seen directly from
Eq. (C29)). Moreover, the commutation described above
does not persist to higher orders: at O(ς2ϵ), for exam-
ple, the parallel part of Z(0) feeds curvature couplings
back into the δz+ equation, in a manner analogous to the
κ δB∥term in Eq. (18), together with other corrections
of the same order. This is entirely expected, but sug-
gests that more extreme orderings — for example those
allowing even larger outward amplitudes or larger per-
pendicular gradients — would be interesting to explore
in future work to understand such effects.
2.
Equilibrium and transport in the transonic
ordering
A complete transport-order derivation is much less
clean in the transonic ordering than in the standard ex-
pansion.
Because U ∼ςvA and p ∼ς2B2, the sepa-
ration between equilibrium and transport is no longer
sharply defined: the mean thermal-pressure force, the
wave-pressure force associated with |B + B(0)|2, and
the quadratic fluctuation stresses all enter over a nearby
range of orders, including in the perpendicular balance.
Likewise, there is no unique slow ordering for all mean
fields, because transport-time derivatives can naturally
appear at different orders for different quantities.
For
this reason, along with the system’s corresponding lack of
a clear energy-conservation structure, we do not attempt
here a full transport derivation analogous to App. A 5.
What does remain robust are the turbulent fluxes. As
in the standard theory, a turbulence average over a re-
gion whose size is comparable to the background-gradient
scale adds one power of ϵ to any averaged divergence,
so the transport equations are naturally controlled by
quadratic-fluctuation correlators at O(ϵ2ς). For example,
the density flux ⟨ρu⟩gives the lowest-order contribution
ρ ⟨Z(0) δρ/ρ⟩/2, giving exactly the same slaved-closure
structure used in the main text.
The magnetic trans-
port is likewise governed by the same physics, yielding


## Page 67


67
the result that there is no parallel EMF at lowest rele-
vant order, and thus only advective rather than diffusive
magnetic transport: u×B ∝Z+×Z−so that the leading
parallel piece is ⟨Z(0) × δz−
⊥⟩; because δz−
⊥remains per-
pendicular and divergence free, the same argument made
in App. A 5 d holds. More generally, for a pure outward
Alfv´enic carrier, the leading ς2 pieces of ρuu −BB/4π
cancel, so the surviving cross-field transport requires the
O(ϵ) corrections, just as in our slaved closures — there
are no new dominant transport contributions.
The practical outcome is that the same slaved phe-
nomenological heating and transport closures used in
the main text should remain appropriate in this regime,
even though their asymptotic placement within a trans-
port hierarchy is less clean. In particular, the turbulent
Reynolds–Maxwell stresses, density fluxes, magnetic-flux
transport, and heat fluxes retain the same local corre-
lation structure, so the leading perpendicular transport
and heating channels are unchanged from the standard
ordering. What changes is the status of the mean evolu-
tion equations: because W +
⊥∼p ∼ρU 2 in a transonic,
low-β, strongly wave-driven wind, wave-pressure forces
and wave-energy fluxes can enter at the same order as
the equilibrium force balance, while the slow-time deriva-
tives of some mean quantities are pushed to higher order.
The leading mean equations are therefore more naturally
interpreted as quasi-steady, wave-supported balance con-
ditions, with time-dependent transport appearing only
after an additional slow ordering is specified. This com-
plicates the construction of a clean, energy-conserving
transport theory analogous to App. A 5.
In this sense, the present subsidiary expansion is nat-
urally connected to the resummed-equilibrium viewpoint
discussed in App. B: the fluctuation equations and their
closure objects remain robust, but equilibrium, wave-
pressure, wave-action, and heating terms may move be-
tween different formal orders, or drop out, depending on
the local asymptotic regime. The resummed approach
is then to retain those terms that can be comparable
somewhere in the domain, rather than assigning a single
formal order globally. Where the low-β or subsonic limits
are well satisfied, the formally smaller terms then become
numerically negligible automatically; where the wind is
instead close to a quasi-steady, wave-supported state, the
slow-time derivatives may be the small quantities, leav-
ing the retained wave-pressure and heating terms to enter
the leading balance.
Appendix D: Ideal MHD instabilities from the
generalized RMHD system
A useful feature of the multiscale RMHD system de-
rived in App. A is that, in the same spirit as local gy-
rokinetics, arbitrary background curvature and perpen-
dicular gradients enter the fluctuation equations only as
coefficients. One may therefore analyze a wide range of
standard ideal-MHD instabilities with little additional ef-
fort by linearizing the reduced system about a given lo-
cal equilibrium, then specializing to appropriate choices
of κ, K⊥
B, K⊥
p , K⊥
ρ , K⊥
U, and geff. As well as providing
an algebraically simple method that can be readily gen-
eralized to kinetic instabilities also, this could help for
general understanding and analysis of instabilities, facil-
itating their categorization into classes driven by similar
physics. In this appendix, we do not attempt such an
exhaustive analysis, but simply record the local linear
system and use it to recover a number of familiar limits.
We start from the RMHD equations in the potential
form (A38), (A43)–(A45), together with δu⊥= ˆb × ∇Φ
and δB⊥/√4πρ = ˆb × ∇Ψ, as this results in the most
natural and physically intuitive formulation of the linear
system. We neglect dissipation, and, for simplicity, set
all background parallel gradients to zero,

the coefficients vary along the field line and the local
algebraic dispersion relation is replaced by a field-line-
following eigenvalue problem, as in ideal-MHD balloon-
ing theory [e.g., 207]. We will also assume, purely for
the sake of simplicity, that all background perpendicular
variation lies in the same plane. The setup captures most
situations that are homogeneous along the local magnetic
field, including curvature- and stratification-driven cou-
plings relevant to buoyancy, Parker, interchange, kink,
and magnetorotational-like modes. It excludes reflection
effects from K∥
vA or wind expansion, as well as the par-
allel variation of B needed for ballooning-like instabili-
ties in closed-field-line configurations [208]. The standard
Kelvin-Helmholtz instability, with k∥∼k⊥, is not cap-
tured by the system, due to the original assumption of
anisotropy. With appropriate generalizations to include
dissipation, the same local framework could also capture
resistive tearing modes [123, 209] and double-diffusive in-
stabilities [210, 211].
Mathematically, these assumptions naturally lead to a

The shearless assumption is important here.
Once

within the present framework, but one no longer has a
simple algebraic dispersion relation: instead, the prob-
lem becomes an ODE/eigenvalue problem along the field,
with singular structure near resonant surfaces.
Those
more general cases are treated extensively in Goedbloed

Tayler-like instabilities for stellar-interior applications
provide another natural extension of the present analy-
sis. In stellar interiors, predominantly toroidal fields are
generically unstable to non-axisymmetric m = 1 modes
but modified by rotation, buoyancy, shear, and other ef-
fects. The same local operator used above should provide
a convenient starting point for studying such hybrid in-
stabilities within an algebraically simple framework [216–
218].

different character, is a rotating, curved-field equilibrium
appropriate to a local patch of an accretion disc. We take
the field to lie in the azimuthal-vertical plane, with dom-
inant gradients and curvature in the radial direction R.

The main point for the present paper is not this stan-
dard limit itself, but that it emerges straightforwardly
from the same reduced operator used above for other
modes that are generally considered in a very differ-
ent context, namely Parker and interchange. The same
framework therefore also provides an immediate start-
ing point for more general disk instabilities, including
disks with strong pressure support and non-Keplerian ro-
tation, strong-field modifications of the MRI [221–223],
and mixed Parker–MRI behaviour in vertically stratified
disk atmospheres/coronae [e.g., 224, 225].
Appendix E: Comparison to multiscale gyrokinetics
for magnetic-confinement fusion
In this appendix, we demonstrate that the govern-
ing equations derived in App. A can be recovered, in
certain limits, via a subsidiary expansion of the multi-
scale gyrokinetic system, originally developed for study-
ing turbulence and transport in the context of magnetic-
confinement fusion [43, 68, 226].
This not only serves
as a consistency check of the expressions derived here,
but also allows us to clarify how features and effects that
arise in the closed-flux-surface geometry assumed for fu-
sion applications manifests in the coordinate-free setting
used here.
It is likewise our hope that the vast con-
ceptual simplification provided by a fluid over a kinetic
model could prove useful for qualitatively understanding
aspects of fusion turbulent transport.
The remainder of this appendix is organised as fol-
lows. After briefly introducing the gyrokinetic system of
equations and notation (§E 1), we outline the subsidiary
ordering that will form the basis for our asymptotic ex-
pansion thereof (§E 2). We then show that both the gen-
eralized RMHD equations (§ E 3) and the associated set
of transport equations (§ E 4) can be recovered directly


## Page 71


71
from within this subsidiary expansion. Readers already
familiar with gyrokinetic theory may wish to skip ahead
to §E 2, working backwards where further clarification is
required.
1.
Local δf gyrokinetics
Many studies of plasma turbulence in magnetic-
confinement fusion are conducted within the framework
of local δf gyrokinetics, which describes the evolution
of highly-anisotropic, small amplitude fluctuations in the
presence of an equilibrium magnetic field. Here, the word
‘local’ refers to the fact that there is an assumed scale
separation between the characteristic scales of the turbu-
lence and those of the equilibrium, an entirely analogous
setup to that considered in this present work. The fluc-
tuations are assumed to obey the standard gyrokinetic
ordering [43]:

While the choice of (E14) can simply be regarded as
an ansatz in order to simplify the derivation presented
here, it can be made exact in the following way. Expand-
ing all quantities to leading-order in (E10), one obtains
kinetic dynamical equations for large-scale dynamics —
so-called “kinetic reduced MHD” (KRMHD), but includ-
ing variation of the equilibrium quantities unlike stan-
dard derivations [69]. These equations, however, depend
both on higher-order moments of the distribution func-
tion (through, e.g., the perturbed perpendicular and par-
allel heat fluxes) as well as its anisotropy (e.g., through
the difference between the parallel and perpendicular
pressures).
To allow for a direct comparison with the
equations derived in App. A, one must eliminate these
terms by taking the collisional limit ω/νss′ →0, en-
forcing the perturbed Maxwellian solution (E14) (note
that this subsidiary ordering in collisionality cannot be
done simultaneously with the large-scale one (E10) as
this leads to different equations that are not relevant to
the present study). Given that the purpose of this ap-
pendix is to illustrate the connection to the gyrokinetic
limit, we choose to simplify the derivation by adopting
the ansatz (E14) from the outset, acknowledging these
subtleties in taking the collisional limit.

recover the equations of generalized RMHD derived in

We have thus shown that an appropriate subset of
the generalized RMHD equations that were derived in
App. A 4 can also be obtained via a direct expansion of
the gyrokinetic system of equations under the assump-
tion of a Maxwellian distribution function.
4.
Recovering the transport equations
We now show that the form of the transport equations
derived in §A 5 j can be recovered from the standard form
of the transport equations adopted in transport theories
of gyrokinetics [41–43].
We first briefly introduce the
flux-surface average in § E 4 a (in particular, its relation
to our average and flux-following time derivative), before
considering the density and thermal energy transport in
sections §E 4 b and §E 4 c, respectively. We will not con-
sider momentum transport here since we are neglecting
equilibrium flows for the purposes of this comparison.
a.
The flux-surface average
As mentioned at the end of § E 1, equilibrium quanti-
ties such as the density ns and temperature Ts are func-
tions of only the flux-surface coordinate ψ, often taken
to be either the poloidal or toroidal magnetic flux in the
toroidal geometries relevant to magnetic-confinement fu-
sion contexts. This means that the relevant transport of
these quantities is flux-surface perpendicular and in the
flux-surface-following frame, viz., the frame that moves
at the velocity eVψ (see Eq. (A103) and preceding text).
Fusion gyrokinetic theories deal with this by defining the
flux-surface average [43, 229, 230],
⟨. . .⟩ψ = lim
∆ψ→0

energy flux.
The simplicity of the source in this limit
should be understood as resulting from the lack of other
energy reservoirs with which to exchange energy: dis-
sipation of large-scale thermal gradients via turbulence
cannot, ultimately, heat the plasma (though any fluc-
tuations that driven and dissipated will still cause heat
transport), meaning that the only true heating is via en-
ergy exchange with the background magnetic field. In
this field-following frame, the latter manifests as a com-
pressional heating via ∇· eVψ.

[84] The proof is straightforward using the potential form for
z±. Compute ∇× (z+ · A) = ∇× (ˆb × ∇ζ+ · A), which
gives −ˆb A : ∇∇ζ+ to lowest order. The ∇∇operator is
symmetric, showing that A : ∇∇ζ+ = 0, and thus that
z+ · A is a purely compressive perpendicular forcing on
z−.

[127] Advection by the magnetic field in the compressive
equations accounts for propagation of fluctuations along
the true (wandering) field lines caused by z+ fluctu-
ations. Field-line tangling will generate small perpen-
dicular scales and thus act as an effective cascade in
both k⊥and k∥(the latter via reconnection of field
lines enabled by dissipation [95]). If such terms domi-
nate, then the interpretation of Eqs. (45) must be mod-
ified, with, for example, FB becoming more relevant
to δu∥, and vice versa. In the slow-mode equations,
the ratio of this δB⊥advection to that from δu⊥is
≃(vS/vA)δ ˜V∥/δu∥for δu∥, or ≃(vS/vA)δu∥/δ ˜V∥for
δ ˜V∥, and since vS/vA ∼β1/2 at β ≪1, the effect should
be subdominant in the β ≪1 corona for at least one of
δu∥and δ ˜V∥. Since we generally care about whichever
of δu∥and δ ˜V∥is larger, this justifies its neglect. In the
δρ equation, perpendicular advection yields a turbulent
balance for δu∥rather than δρ, with the interpretation
that parallel compressions locally balance z+ forcing,
meaning (47) remains an appropriate estimate for δρ.

[204] A reader may reasonably ask why we should preference
the parallel equilibrium equation in this way, as opposed
to the perpendicular one. The answer is that one could,
if desired, also sum the perpendicular force balance at
O(ϵ) and O(ϵ3). The result is simply Eq. (A100), which
captures both the mean perpendicular forces and mean
turbulent stresses (Reynolds/Maxwell stresses and tur-
bulent pressure) — if this were solved as a single equa-
tion, rather than separately enforced at first and third
orders, the equilibrium would also include such turbu-
lent stresses. However, such corrections, unlike the par-
allel ones, are likely not of significant importance, and
care would be needed to ensure energy conservation and
that ensure fast timescales are not inadvertently intro-
duced into the system.
